ParlaMint-ES_2020-05-20-CD200520.u225	Thank you very much, President. Your Honor, as you have just said, the Canarian Community is facing a very complicated situation as a result of the coronavirus. It is a community different from the others for obvious reasons related to its geographical location and its high dependence on tourism, as you have pointed out, which accounts for 35 % of economic activity and 40 % of occupation. This fact makes it very directly impacted from the very beginning because it is this sector most immediately affected by restrictions on international mobility and restrictions on mobility in general by citizens. We are perfectly aware of this particular and difficult thesis in which your autonomous community is located. We are not indifferent at all; on the contrary, we are firmly committed. That is why, from the outset, we have acted decisively so that the crisis - certain - does not mean, as you suggested, the breakdown of an economic model. I prefer not to use hyperbolic or similar expressions that seem to me not very appropriate in the health crisis situation we are experiencing. I believe that the Government's work should focus on saving, saving lives, saving businesses, saving jobs and saving sectors that are essential to our country, such as tourism, and that is where we are. In this regard, let me remind you that the first measure launched by the Government was precisely for the tourism sector with the extension of the Official Credit Institute line up to EUR 400 million that we created last autumn after the collapse of Thomas Cook. Also then our reaction was immediate, agile, rapid and proportionate to respond to an unexpected situation that particularly damaged its autonomous community. This line, which has already been laid down in its entirety, has made it possible to finance more than 200 operations in the Canary Islands worth EUR 23 million and has played an important role in channelling liquidity immediately. But, without doubt, the most powerful measure to maintain the productive fabric in this extraordinary period is the line of up to 100 billion euros of endorsements that the ICO is deploying; there are three sections already deployed and the fourth has been approved yesterday in the Council of Ministers. According to the available data, until 18 May the ICO had approved 1607 million euros in 17,614 operations in the Canary Islands, which means that 13,641 companies in the Canary Islands have received almost 2110 million euros of financing with the approval of the State. The tourism sector in general is the most important in the funding that ICO is providing, not only in the Canary Islands but throughout the national territory, with approximately 17% of the total amount of guarantees and the total funding. I am not surprised, as, as we have pointed out, it is the sector most affected. Similarly, temporary employment regulations and the special allowance for self-employed persons have benefited notably from some tourist activities such as hospitality, where up to 82% of the workers affiliated throughout the country are covered by one of these measures. And it is very positive that we signed a week ago to extend the use of these ERTs during the period of the state of alarm, as well as to establish a procedure for the possible extension of this extraordinary mechanism, which involves important budgetary support from all Spanish taxpayers. I know that the Minister of Labour is already setting up a tripartite table to analyse how we are going to adapt this extraordinary system of support for ERTs in the most affected sectors, and obviously tourism is one of those that comes to mind immediately. This means that all workers are being protected with unemployment benefits, that this safety net has been extended for all workers, that we are seeing how to adapt the extraordinary mechanisms of the crisis to accompany the process of descalation and support economic recovery, not to mention the other measures of postponement of tax payments, of extraordinary benefit for cessation of activity, etc. I think more than just talking about ransoms than what we need to talk about is building the future together. That is why the Government has proposed a social and economic reconstruction pact that we have offered to the autonomous communities, to the political parties, to the social actors, to society in the end, which I hope we will have an opportunity to discuss or in whose framework we will have an opportunity to discuss shortly, and which requires the help of all, we must all help to build this plan, because we have to think about the future, and that necessarily means thinking first of all about the tourism sector. We must try to boost as soon as possible the return of national and international visitors. That is why work has already begun in the Tourism Sectoral Commission on a re-launching plan that includes health safety elements. In fact, the Ministry of Health has issued various orders to try to establish as soon as possible a safe operating system for the reopening of hotels, tourist accommodation, swimming pools or beaches in the second phase of the descalade. Part of the territory of the Canary Islands is already at that stage or will be very soon at that stage, which will enable all these reactivation measures to be implemented. And these are tremendously important measures, because I believe that your honor will agree with me on the crucial importance of regaining confidence in the Spanish tourism sector, recovering economic activity in a framework that can guarantee the safety and health of all people who come to our wonderful enclaves. You have pointed out the importance of coordination with our European partners - this would be the second chapter that I think is fundamental in this context - and it is that the countries of the Union are among the main visitors of our country, are key to the tourism sector in general and especially to the tourism sector in the Canary Islands. In this regard, nine countries, including Spain, presented a joint statement at the last informal meeting of tourism ministers requesting a recovery plan from the European Union to alleviate the negative impact of the pandemic on the European tourism sector. The aim is to establish uniform and shared protocols by all Member States, to allocate sufficient funds to be able to recover activity and to support businesses and workers in the sector in the future recovery plan of the European Union, and to address the specific problems of island or outermost regions. In this regard, the Spanish Government is firmly committed and working very actively to ensure that tourism plays a key role in all that is the plan for reconstruction and future economic recovery that is being built at European level. And I will now turn to two specific issues which are particularly important in this immediate future. I have already mentioned the importance of common security protocols. We also need common protocols on the opening of borders, because we are convinced that it is the only way to maintain the unity of the single European area at this difficult time, without prejudice of course to the fact that each country can adapt the rules to its specificities and the stage of descalation in which it finds itself, according to its epidemiological data. In fact, in this context, last week the European Commission published a package of guidelines and recommendations on how to gradually withdraw travel restrictions, in order to move towards a return to progressive normality that will save part of this summer campaign for tourism. I would point this out to you because a few days ago you expressed the importance you attached to safety in all aspects of traffic, and in particular air transport, which is so important to the Community of the Canary Islands. We are following this whole process very closely at European level so that our country is very aligned with the international recommendations, and we can, as I said, recover that flow of tourists as soon as possible. Beyond the topic of common protocols in the physical field and of transport safety, there is another area where we are also working actively at European and national level, the development of mechanisms for the tracing of contacts, the use of apps, which are being developed in different areas to try to have additional tools to control a possible outbreak of the epidemic. I am aware that the Canarian Government is very interested in having these mechanisms as soon as possible, being key for Spain to use technologies, to use interoperable apps, so that anyone from anywhere in the Union can feel safe and participate in the system of monitoring the tracing of contacts and the possible expansion of the epidemic, in order, in short, as I always say, to recover tourism safely. That is why we are working actively with the Government of the Autonomous Community of the Canary Islands to develop the first pilot there for the establishment of this health system support app, an app that must, of course, be perfectly respectful of individual rights and the protection of privacy and privacy of people, and that must have added value and support the safety and monitoring network for the possible expansion of the Canarian health system virus. Canary Islands is ideal for the importance of its tourism sector, for the importance of using these apps to make tourists feel safe in our country, beyond the descalade, and it is also ideal because it has an autonomous government very committed to that safe descalade, and with which we are collaborating very directly. ParlaMint-ES_2020-05-20-CD200520.u232	Thank you very much. Good afternoon. The situation of the treasury of the municipalities of Spain is well known, and it is a recurring matter already for several years, but in these months it has been placed in the first line of the outstanding matters and they do not finish closing. This decision has caused a deep discomfort in most of the municipalities affected, and this discomfort has been increasing because, despite having formed a commission formed by the Federation of Municipalities and Provinces of the Autonomous Communities and the FEMP itself, it has not met lately and that I know already for at least two or three weeks without being convened. Although there was a meeting of the President of the Government with the Federation of Municipalities and Provinces, you are the representative of the matter and you have to answer on this matter. I imagine that you must be in permanent contact with Abel Caballero, the president of the FEMP, and that you will have talked about something new about the requests of the municipalities, but the fact is that none of the federations of municipalities of the autonomous communities have news, and he had an order to continue to negotiate with the government on behalf of the FEMP, but nothing is known. That is why I ask you to explain here the scope of those talks, which you have had with him, if there has been any, because at the moment the position of the municipalities is very much in favour of making their own funds available with complete freedom in the present circumstances. We are all aware that at this time it is not possible for the regulation of financial sustainability and because we need to have an overall view, but circumstances have already changed in a radical way. To begin with, there is no longer a limitation on deficit and debt on the part of the European Union, and this completely changes things for the Spanish Government. To this day, which seems to be the health issue, the two great challenges we have are to meet the people who are having a really bad time, who don't even have to eat - and they are the people closest to the municipalities - and to help those who are seeing their economy, their own savings are going to the bottom and need to resume economic activity as soon as possible; many of them are going out of unemployment and others are small entrepreneurs, SMEs and self-employed. To combat all this the municipal treasury purse is like a gift fallen from the sky, because we are talking about a very important amount, of the order of 28 billion euros, according to the latest calculations that have been made public. We have to make a decision now, especially the Government: do we want a quick recovery or a slow recovery? And I say this because it is repeated a lot - and I share it - that we have to go to a productive model change, but then I will say something about that. In any case, what is unanimous is that we have to act on economic reconstruction as soon as possible, and it is obvious that we have to act at all levels, starting with Europe, which is doing a lot of begging. They talked about the last investment of half a billion euros, but they had also talked about one before and everything is being delayed. In Spain too, this plan will be delayed for a few months. Many autonomous communities have already begun - in Cantabria there is a shock plan fundamentally to alleviate the damage it is causing - and the municipalities are also moving, because they have the closest need - do not reach Congress, but the mayor is called to the door - but much less than they need. That's why you have to make shock plans at all levels. Each has its own field and the municipalities are probably the fastest to act. I will try to be constructive and try to contribute something to this debate. The problem is that when you get out here, as it's all being recorded and there's TV, you have to be very careful about what you say and not screw up, because sometimes you have to say what our voters want and not risk any controversial issues. My proposal is to allow the municipalities to devote most of this savings, that of the last eight years - not only that of the 2019 surplus - to investments, and in particular to public works and subsidies to neighbours for the rehabilitation of housing, which is in line with the ideas of the European Union. We are going to put aside at this moment the construction of large infrastructures to fight climate change, to prevent disasters such as floods, droughts, earthquakes; infrastructures that, as Jeremy Rifkin said, serve us to live in a different way, what is called the Green New Deal. These include large energy, communications, transport and logistics infrastructure. This is what the European Union recommends. But the controversial issue and what affects us here in the short term is that, in recent times, there has been a tendency in Spain to demonise the construction sector and, in particular, the companies in the sector. I myself have often suffered accusations of supporting public works companies when I was a consultant to the branch, because there are people who think that what needs to be done is to pursue contractors because they are dockers, but logically I think that the rulers, especially those who are dedicated to public works, support these types of companies, and I say to everyone, the big ones, the small ones and the medium ones. In fact, the big ones are pioneers of the big companies in the world. Today I want to defend here the need to apply the Keynesian recipe, but without going to the extreme when it said, in its general theory, that what had to be done was to bury tickets and get companies to take them out, because that generated economic activity and generated employment. It is not about that, but the important thing is to put the machinery to work to create employment and recover the production chain. That's not my main argument, is it? Economic activity in a country is full of networks of millions of commercial and industrial relations between all, businesses and citizens, and this goes from the highest level, from the level of industry to the lowest level, that of citizens. Now it's up to the public administrations to act, and on the scale that each of the municipalities has to play a key role in these small works that, in addition, are the ones that their neighbors want. And I'm going back to a question I asked at first. Do we want a quick recovery or do we want a slow recovery? Because you really have to decide what the role is. It is clear that in such a globalised world we will follow the recovery that the rest of Europe has, and if Europe goes faster, so will we, although we will go a little slower because it seems that it is doing us more harm. But it is clear that we have a plot of our own on which we can act, and there the drag has to be noticed, but the fundamental thing is that in what is in our own hands we act. A short-term model change is not possible and this is something we all need to know. We have to take advantage of our strengths because of course we are not going to start making chips or scanners overnight, but what we do know is to manufacture, build roads, squares, public lights, all these small works that will continue to exist and will continue to be necessary forever. In addition, today we are in a very different situation than in 2007, where there was a big bubble; as an example, in Cantabria, the construction sector increased from about 40,000 employed to about 10,000, a share has been recovered, but not much. So, right now, we don't have to have any fear of bubbles or anything like that, what it's all about is reassembling the economy. And in all this micro-economy of peoples and families, in the end, there are relationships that are in an unstable balance, and what can't be done is to suddenly change the macro-economy, but in the small one it can act, and this is what I want to propose to you, that an important shock plan be made for the public work. It's fast, it generates a lot of activity and it also generates one thing that I suppose would be very good for you, and it is that it generates a return to the public coffers of the order of 50% and with the drop of collection that I suppose will be there, and that you will know, look at how well these entries would come in the treasury of something that is not producing anything. I am not saying that they spend the 28 billion, but an important part should be taken into account. The Government of Cantabria has now made a small plan in the councilhouse for municipalities to devote it to lighting, roads, all water cycle-related works and which also recommends Europe, sanitation, supplies, purification and things related, and much, to climate change. Mrs Von der Leyen also said that this shock plan must be combined with the green pact and the Digital Agenda. But today we are talking about something very home, very close: the municipalities. There are eight thousand municipalities in Spain, many of which have surpluses, and I think they need to have this kind of activity. In addition, capital investments, taking savings to capital or even debt at least balance your assets and liabilities, it's not like when you go straight to consumption, which eventually disappears. The multiplier of investment is much greater than consumption, because in the end it also comes to consumption, then we have to go through this process. At the moment we are already with a series of measures that are what they have to take and are taking the Government of the nation and many autonomous communities, but we will complete it with this other kind of investment. I think what I'm saying here is not new, there are a lot of people who are going to say it, but it's fundamental if we want to see things in the medium term. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES_2020-05-20-CD200520.u241	Thank you very much, President. Madam Minister, you may find this inquiry to be repetitive, but, look, the three appeals lodged this afternoon by three different political groups and three different territories are calling for concrete action with regard to the possibility of using the treasury remnants of local corporations and municipalities. Remnants of treasury, which are the closest thing to the operating fund of any company or the savings accumulated over the last few years, are of a significant amount. The latest published data is from 2018 and amounted to 17,841 million euros that of the Spanish state as a whole, of the local corporations. I already know that the 2019 surplus is 3839 million euros. You'll want to talk more about the surplus and now I want to talk more about the remnants. At the moment, Madam Minister, it is imperative that the municipalities, which are adult and responsible administrations, can freely use this remnant. I want you to come up here today and say it clearly or, if not, to say: no, you will not be able to use it freely. And I would also like you to use it to dispel all these - I don't know what they are - probe balloons or bubbles, because it is true that some of your speeches in the Committee on Finance or the intervention of the Minister for Territorial Policy, last Thursday in this House, cast some doubt. Even when you say here that the remnants are not of the municipalities, but of the citizens, I have to say: yes, yes, but they are of the citizens of that municipality. Look, what are these remnants of fruit? For they are the result of increased fiscal pressure from some municipalities on others; they are the result of lower expenditure from some municipalities on others. Therefore, I understand that you ask me what we do with the citizens of the municipalities that have no remnants. For we will have to study it, but it must favor those who have generated it, the citizens of that municipality, because if at some point - I think it should not be so and I would like you to come up here to deny it - a mutualization of the remnants of treasury would be an absolute disincentive for a good management of each of the local corporations. I'm sure it won't, and I'd like you to come up here and say that the mutualization of the treasury remnants is a fake new, a bubble, and there's no need to be afraid. Each may use its remnants. What do I ask of you? That they can be used in social spending, 20% - I would not put any restrictions on them, Minister - and also in financially sustainable investments because - we talked about a moment ago - investments at local level, investments in public works, have on local economies a tractor factor that is very relevant in some municipalities and should therefore be able to use them. They can also be used for employment plans at the local level or to help small businesses, self-employed people, and municipalities will already decide what is a priority at every moment because they are adult administrations, beyond protecting them under article 140 of the Constitution establishing their autonomy, municipal autonomy. And don't worry, the mayors are aware that next year is going to be very difficult, of course they are, as they are aware that they are going to have a significant reduction in their income in the 2021 fiscal year. They will already reserve them in the proportion they deem appropriate. We ask you to use these remnants, obviously, without them computing for the spending rule. It wouldn't make sense. This already happened with financially sustainable investments and has happened with 20%, but not with the rest of the remnants. And most importantly, Minister - it is something that creates problems for many municipalities - which also does not count for establishing budgetary stability. We only ask for the 2020 and 2021 years. This is feasible because, according to article 11 of the Budget Stability Act, the autonomous communities and the General Administration of the State, in the face of exceptional situations such as the one we are living in, can obviate it; instead, local corporations cannot. I no longer ask you to avoid it; the only thing I ask you to do is to ensure that this budgetary stability is not affected by the application of the rest of the treasury. In addition, we need concreteness and timetable, Minister. Mayors and mayors need calendars. They are pending every Tuesday whether there is a royal decree or not; tell them what they want to do and give them a calendar. Minister, there are other aspects of the local administration that concern us. As they have done for the General Administration of the State and Social Security, they would have to try to give the possibility of deferring municipal taxes without interest of delay. Article 10 of the Law Regulating Local Finances provides for it with two restrictions: that there must be an ordinance that regulates it - at present many municipalities do not have it and, if they have to process it, no longer arrive in time - and that debts have to be paid in full in the current year. We ask you, as many municipalities ask, that the postponement may be extended beyond the current exercise, because there are some municipalities that can allow it quietly because they have no debt and have high remnants, and this is another element to be considered. Madam Minister, with regard to the financing of local corporations, I would like to raise another point: the European funds. When European funds arrive, whether they are non-return funds or loan funds, they must have territorial permeability. One part will have to reach the autonomous communities, but another part we understand that it also has to reach the municipalities directly. They have capacity, they have confidence; the municipalities have been the only administrations that have generated surplus over the past few years. You, before your mayors, have promised to change it, now is the opportunity to avoid situations such as those that are taking place, in which certain aid policies, for example, are informed favorably by interveners in a municipality and unfavourablely by other interveners in the municipality next door. Let us make it possible for harmonization, for there is no interpretation and for the law to be clear about what can be done and what cannot be done. We can't offer these mayors any more uncertainty. And with all my affection, I'm telling you not to rest on the FEMP. ParlaMint-ES_2020-05-20-CD200520.u251	We continue with the point corresponding to the validation or repeal of royal decree-laws. Validation or repeal of Royal Decree-Law 17/2020 of 5 May, approving measures to support the cultural and tax sector to address the economic and social impact of COVID-2019. To present the royal decree-law, the Minister of Culture and Sport has the floor on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2020-05-20-CD200520.u252	Thank you very much, President. With the venom. Ladies and gentlemen, I bring to this House for validation the Royal Decree-Law 17/2020 of 5 May, approving measures to support the cultural sector to address the economic and social impact of COVID-19. Ladies and gentlemen, as you know, on 11 March the World Health Organization brought an unprecedented health crisis to the international pandemic, which has spread extremely rapidly and has affected more than 4 million people around the world. I know that we all feel it is an inescapable and profound duty to pay a sincere and respectful tribute to those who have not been able to overcome this cruel pandemic, to the nearly 28 000 victims that have left us in our country, as well as to the close to 320,000 human beings who have lost their lives throughout the world. The extraordinary risk and aggressiveness of the pandemic has led us in these weeks to take a series of measures to deal with the situation immediately. The state of alarm has allowed the guaranteed limitation of our mobility in order to combat the virus effectively and at the same time has been accompanied by a set of decisions taken in the Council of Ministers and in this House to alleviate the very important economic and social impact of COVID-19, also and in a particularly acute way - we are all perfectly aware - among the cultural industries. Following the health priority, the Government has at no time lost sight of the reactivation of our companies and sectors, taking measures to maintain the productive fabric in order to minimize impact, facilitate economic recovery and protect jobs. I am convinced that this has also been the will and willingness of all public administrations, autonomous communities and municipalities. The temporary cessation of activity and consumption also reached the culture. It is one of the particularly damaged economic sectors. The cultural and scenic spaces were drastically paralyzed and with them the activity of a professional group already characterized in some of its sectors by an extraordinary structural fragility. Ladies and gentlemen, from the government we consider it essential to implement the measures contained in this royal decree-law, and we think this because of the uniqueness of the cultural sector, but also because of its per se, qualitative value, in itself. Culture is one of the pillars of our social and democratic rule of law. We know that the weight of culture in the Spanish economy is significant, with a contribution of 3.2% to the gross domestic product. In 2019, the volume of cultural employment amounted to more than 700,000 people, 3.6% of total employment in Spain. The public authorities must promote - as mandated by the 1978 Constitution - access to culture and its universal enjoyment. That is why we must protect and take care of the protagonists of the cultural fact. Culture is a backbone of our democracy. It is our obligation to ensure the survival of cultural structures and to protect employment and businesses operating in the sector. They are people and they are values, rights and principles. I'm sure they share this appreciation with me. From the Ministry of Culture and Sport we have worked intensively to alleviate the impact that this unprecedented crisis is having on the cultural sector, aggravating a situation characterized historically and in many cases by precariousness. We therefore begin to adapt the economic measures of a cross-cutting nature adopted on 17 March to the specificities of the cultural sector through different adjustments and then continue with the adoption of more concrete measures, aimed in this case at alleviating the situation of extraordinary and urgent need that passes through the cultural sector and to help in the process of transition, called de-escalation, towards the new normality. Taking this context into account, the present Royal Decree focuses on supporting businesses and cultural workers, with specific measures to respond to the needs arising from the exceptional situation created by the health crisis. Thus, the circumstances of extraordinary and urgent need required by article 86 of the Spanish Constitution are clearly present. Given the general interest on which the measures are based, the Royal Decree-Law is undoubtedly the most appropriate instrument to ensure their achievement. Allow me now, ladies and gentlemen, to begin to analyse the main contents of the decree whose validation is subject to your consideration. First, the Royal Decree-Law deals with general measures to support companies in the cultural sector. The Government has put into operation the ICO guarantee mechanism for the financing of companies, which is successfully meeting its overall target of EUR 80 billion, already taking into account the last decision in the Council of Ministers last Tuesday. However, they have not reached companies in the sector and other economic sectors of the country with the desired intensity. For this reason, it has been essential to make use of a financial tool specialized in providing guarantees to small and medium-sized enterprises in the Spanish cultural sector, the CreA Mutual Guarantee Society, to which we provide funds totalling EUR 20 million. This amount actually involves mobilising EUR 700 million in guaranteed loans for the cultural sector, soft loans or under optimal conditions. Each cultural sector will have access to a minimum of €40 million. Thus, the book, the music, the beautiful arts, the performing arts, the dance, also the flamenco, the theater or the circus, of course the cinema, all will be able to obtain immediate and specific liquidity. No one doubts the relevance of performing arts and music in the cultural sector, both from the point of view of the business perspective and of the professional fabric and of the dissemination of culture. In line with this, we have set out a set of important measures for the sector. Special emphasis is placed on the introduction of an extraordinary support system for the maintenance of its structures and for the development of performing and musical activities or cultural projects. These grants, worth EUR 38.2 million, will be implemented by Inaem, the National Institute of Performing Arts and Music, following the competitive competition procedure and the principles of advertising, transparency, objectivity, equality and non-discrimination. In addition, public procurement legislation is amended to allow cultural professionals to receive advances and compensation in the event of the postponement or cancellation of actions already contracted with the public sector, with a limit of EUR 50 000. Cinematography and the audiovisual arts, in turn, have a multiplier effect on jobs and technical jobs in the sector and have the capacity to project our languages - the different Spanish languages - and our culture beyond our borders. They also attract foreign investment and are a demand for tourism. This part of the industry too, as it cannot be otherwise, receives support measures. Film production has a singularity that makes it particularly sensitive to this situation, since two of its fundamental phases, such as filming and the premiere in the rooms, have been completely paralysed all this time. Precisely the production aid granted by the Ministry is based on the fulfilment of a number of milestones, such as, among other things, the start of filming, the qualification of films or their commercial premiere in cinemas within certain time limits, so that any failure to do so necessarily leads to failure to do so. Given the inability to meet these obligations in the current context, the Royal Decree extends the deadlines and makes it easier to comply with this set of requirements that aid producers had to meet in 2020. In this way, we avoid a fearful and secure situation of non-compliance in the chain which would result in the impossibility of carrying out the projects in the best conditions or the need for the return of the aid. Furthermore, in order to generate liquidity in the producers, part of the aid granted is advanced before the start of the filming - this only benefits the writers - and it is allowed to include among the eligible costs those directly derived from the impact of the crisis. In addition, we include a temporary exemption from the commercial premiere obligation in cinemas until the exhibition rooms are back in operation. Faced with the accumulation of films that cannot be premiered in rooms when they had planned, they are allowed to be premiered commercially through providers of other communication services or platforms, without losing their status as a film worthy of support. At the same time, we provide for the direct granting of aid amounting to EUR 13.2 million to cinematographic exhibition hall holders, whose activity has also been suspended as a result of the crisis. This aid is intended to revive the sector: on the one hand, to alleviate the costs of implementing a risk prevention plan for its workers, while ensuring the protection and safety of the public at their reopening, expenditure such as the disinfection of spaces, the acquisition of protective material, the installation of bulkheads or the placement of specific signals, and, on the other hand, it is also intended to cover the costs related to re-opening and promotion campaigns and the return of the public to cinemas where possible. Moreover, with the aim of promoting private financing in the audiovisual industry, a number of regulatory changes of a fiscal nature are carried out. In particular, tax incentives related to film production of feature films and short films are significantly increased, which will promote greater competitiveness of the sector both in the domestic and international environments. This will allow in the near future to attract more international productions and shoots, encourage co-productions and increase the activity of the audiovisual industry in Spain, giving the right, in the case of feature film producers, to major deductions, provided that at least 50% of the base of the deduction corresponds to expenses incurred in Spanish territory. In the case of international productions, we reduce the limit from EUR 1 million to EUR 200 000 for animation productions and processes of special digital effects, because the current limits are not sufficiently competitive compared to other countries and we must not miss this opportunity, because precisely the animation and special digital effects can continue to take place from home and we are a world power. It is a job that has not stopped, positive to generate employment, with special participation of young people and linked to new technologies. The royal decree-law also covers the sectors of book and contemporary art. Firstly, we have created an extraordinary aid line of EUR 4 million to ensure that the structures of the book-keeping sector and the book supply chain are maintained. These aids are mainly intended for independent bookstores, whose temporary closure has led to a drastic fall in their turnover which could lead to their cessation. Ensuring the survival of independent bookstores is of vital importance to the sector, as its special fragility affects the flow of economic activity throughout the chain: authors, editors, distributors, translators, etc. They are, to a large extent, the wheel that enables the whole editorial activity to be started. The Directorate-General for Book and Reading Development will implement this system of aid, which will be granted in accordance with the competitive competition procedure. By the way, ladies and gentlemen, aid will be added to the reduction in VAT for electronic books and digital newspapers from 21 % to 4 %, an old demand in the sector that is now being implemented. Secondly, the Royal Decree-Law incorporates extraordinary grants worth EUR 1 million for the promotion of contemporary art, which are articulated in two strands. On the one hand, we promote the development of digital innovation projects that promote the diffusion of the visual arts, artistic creation, communication and international diffusion, and, on the other hand, we grant an extraordinary endowment for the acquisition of Spanish contemporary art. Its purpose is to promote the internal market for contemporary art, which for the first time is granted a specific amount independent of the rest of the budget of the Directorate General of Fine Arts, from which it dedicates to the acquisition of pieces for the public collections of the State. The recipients of these measures include visual artists as well as art galleries, critics and curators. All this without prejudice to the fact that they can also opt for liquidity, at least EUR 40 million, and that they are safe recipients of the tax benefits also approved in this royal decree-law on sponsorship. Another group of measures concerns activities subsidized by the Ministry of Culture and Sport that have been cancelled as a result of COVID-19. Thus, it is possible to pay the eligible costs incurred by the beneficiaries of such aid. Similarly, in the field of sport, scholarships and grants to athletes linked to results achieved in competitions that have not been held are extended, thus preventing them from being deprived of these scholarships and grants. To validate this royal decree-law, ladies and gentlemen, is also to improve the conditions for the participation of civil society, solidarity and philanthropy, which are essential in the revival of the sectors of culture and sport at this time. To this end, as they anticipated with regard to the aid of contemporary art, we raised 5 percentage points the deduction provided for donations, amending to this effect Law 49/2002, on the tax regime of non-profit entities and on tax incentives for sponsorship. In the same vein, we also proceed to declare a series of events as events of exceptional public interest. In addition to the symbolic value they have, this legal figure allows for significant tax benefits to be applied to taxpayers participating in the financing of the above-mentioned events, in accordance with the above-mentioned law. The events are as follows, which also fill us with hope at this time thinking about many of them in the coming year: the Berlanga Plan, launched on the occasion of the centenary of the birth of the universal Valencian filmmaker; the Alicante 2021 Programme, the departure of the World Cup of Sailing; Spain guest of honor at the Frankfurt Book Fair in 2021; the Plan to promote opera on the street, the Royal Theatre, or the 175th anniversary of the construction of the Gran Teatre del Liceu. Finally, the deadline for other programmes considered to be of exceptional public interest and which are, in particular, the following: the Programme for the preparation of the Spanish athletes of the Tokyo Olympic Games, already by 2021; the fifth centenary of the expedition of the first round to the world of Fernando de Magallanes and Juan Sebastián Elcano - surely you like this especially, Mr. Díaz -; Andalucía Valderrama Masters, and the year St. Jacobeo 2021. Ladies and gentlemen, this royal decree is aimed at rescuing people and facing a serious socio-economic situation caused by COVID-19 in the world of culture. In an area other than culture and sport, we also consider it necessary to incorporate some measures that I would like to point out to you in order to give a full explanation of the content of the decree, such as the elimination of the fork parameter in the university public price system for the 2020-2021 course, in order to promote access to public higher education for all students. Also, an amendment to Law 9/2017 on Public Sector Contracts, to facilitate the exercise of the functions conferred on the organs of the State and of the autonomous communities or to improve some provision on procurement contained in Royal Decree-Law 8/2020 of 17 March, the first of economic and social measures after the first state of alarm. I referred earlier to the difficulties faced by the collective of authors and artists in accessing an unemployment benefit or a retirement pension. In order to protect the collective and its most vulnerable members, the copyright management entities, with which I will be reunited in the coming days, created long ago the assistance funds. The Royal Decree-Law strengthens this protective action, enabling for two years the general assemblies of these entities to increase the percentage allocated to the realization of care activities. The main commitment of this Government in this crisis is to leave no one behind. I am convinced that we all share this purpose, a moral, political and legal duty in this pandemic context. People, ladies and gentlemen, must be at the centre of our policies. They will agree with me that it would be pointless to oppose measures to protect cultural workers who are going through situations of real helplessness. Indeed, this royal decree-law that we bring to this House today creates extraordinary access to unemployment benefit for cultural workers who had not been protected by the mechanisms established to date. In fact, it is an old historical demand linked to the status of the artist. The intermittent nature of his work causes many of them not to be discharged at the time of the state of alarm statement. The urgency of this measure is such that, without it, more than 6,300 people, artists of public shows, would be left without any coverage. The overall amount of the set of measures articulated in this Royal Decree-Law amounts to €76.5 million, in addition to those derived from the tax incentives mentioned, in what we consider to be a remarkable effort of solidarity with the world of culture at complicated times of resistance and survival. A fully justified effort in line with the high regard that the Government has for the culture of our country and that I am sure it is shared by the whole of this House. Ladies and gentlemen, I am finishing by saying that culture feeds the soul - or the conscience for the laity - but also the body, raises the spirit of the people, but also the gross domestic product of the countries. Culture is consumption and is industry. Article 27 of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights states in its first paragraph that everyone has the right to participate freely in the cultural life of the community, to enjoy the arts and to participate in scientific progress and in the benefits that result from it. And in these difficult weeks, in these difficult times, that universal access to the works of human creation, the best expression of our dignity, has been essential. The films have entertained us in the long days of confinement, the books have helped us to overcome the anguish by our relatives or friends, the music has comforted us in the unbearable loss of some loved one or of our 28,000 compatriots. Culture, in all its manifestations, has accompanied us in solitude and made us stronger, made us wiser, made us better. In its second and final paragraph, the same article of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights states that everyone has the right to the protection of his or her moral and material interests on the basis of the scientific, literary or artistic productions of which he or she is the author. That the flow of culture does not stop, that it flows wider, that it demands that we protect creation, that we make it possible and sustainable. In the balance of both paragraphs we find the cultural progress of our country. We must strengthen the industrial fabric of culture because, in addition to all that said, it is an intelligent investment in the future, and that is a task for all, because culture is for all. I understand, do not worry, that the task of the opposition is to criticize everything that does not suit them and even what does suit them. I am sure that, like all human works, this decree that we present for validation today is not perfect, it is not the rule of absolute justice of Judge Hercules, I would even say it is not fortunately. But these are emergency measures in a context in addition to crisis and scarcity, and their value lies in urgency. If we wait for the many people who will be able to find support in this decree, shelter or impulse, then we may be late, it may be too late. Think about it when you vote, because the vote of your ladies and gentlemen today is essential for all these people. For all these reasons, indeed, in my opinion, for all these good reasons, today I request your support for the validation of this royal decree-law. Eskerrik asko, many graces, many graces, thank you very much, ladies and gentlemen. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2020-05-20-CD200520.u262	Thank you, President. Minister, this is indeed the time to propose a series of urgent measures and solutions to deal with the damage caused so far by COVID-19 and those which it will continue to cause in the future, in order to ensure, among other things, the survival and sustainability of workers in all artistic expressions and in the sector. Therefore, eskerrik asko, thank you all for making available to us from the networks cultural contents, the fruit of your life, work and effort, and sharing it with all and through different platforms. Today we discuss the Royal Decree-Law 17/2020. In general, our assessment is quite positive, and I point out several reasons to support it. First, it has launched action lines through grants and has made use of its protection system, unemployment or unemployment, to care for people in the sector who have seen their income go to zero. They were the first to suffer the consequences of COVID-19, it was right versus help, and this is something important that other autonomous governments do not understand or have not wanted to understand. The decree contains measures for the financing of the cultural sector, measures to support the performing arts and music, measures to support cinematography and audiovisual arts and, among other measures, also for the book and contemporary art sector, which are fine. However, we must also point out the shortcomings, which have them. As well as the measures to facilitate the financing of the cultural sector and the support of its workers through the direct granting of subsidies to the Audiovisual Mutual Guarantee Society, bonds, cater to the different professional sectors, the same is not true with regard to the extraordinary access to unemployment benefit for artists in public performances. There the decree leaves many professionals in the artistic and cultural fields unprotected, since it only applies to those who are discharged into the General Regime of Artists in public shows. Mr Minister, there are many, many technicians, the assistants who have the contractual characteristics, the intermittentness, the temporality, the same consequences also of this crisis and, however, cannot benefit from these aids. We trust that this decree will be used as a bill, so that we can make the necessary contributions to improve the flaws it presents. However, the overall assessment is positive, although it must be borne in mind that the Spanish State is far from other European countries, such as France or Germany, which have taken action to deal with the crisis without abandoning the cultural industry, and that is something that is not being noticed too much around here. I must also point out that in this comparison there are some European countries that better cater to their differentiated cultural realities, to those diverse cultural expressions that enrich - and in the Spanish State there are a few - and in languages other than hegemonic Spanish. In this decree there are no specific measures that address the socio-linguistic and cultural diversity of non-state nations. And this is the example of the spirit of cultural recentralization, whether consciously or unconsciously, but the result is that. We regret that this has not been taken into account, and that is another reason why we cannot vote in favour of this decree; nor are we going to do it against, because, as I have already said, we believe it contains positive elements for the sector and for the cultural industry, in general, and the picture is rather precarious already. But it is not acceptable that, once again, the measures designed solely from this kind of Castilizing Myopia should be considered, and the cultural diversity existing in the Spanish State should not be taken into account. I am sure that we will all remember this confinement; we will remember with whom we have passed it or in the absence of whom we have passed it, but we will also remember all the works that have accompanied us: the books we have read, the series we have seen, the songs we have heard. Juan Gelman accompanied me, and by reading this poem I could not help but think how current it is in this poem, how important it is to decorate our poets, our filmmakers, musicians, actors, actresses and, also, those most anonymous people who have already been quoted: technicians and techniques, for example, whose names are mostly unknown, but who do an essential job. But the best decoration, the only one, if I hurry, is the right to a dignified life, and that is precisely why this royal decree goes, to put the necessary measures to ensure that the professionals of culture can lead a dignified life in the midst of the blow of this pandemic. There was no doubt that specific measures were needed for culture, because of the specificities of the sector. It has already been said: many cultural professionals were unable to access the generalist aid already approved. This is a government that listens and has also listened to the cultural sector, which clearly claimed that specific measures were needed. It is fair to give the Government that listening and listening capacity, but it is also fair to acknowledge that this royal decree would not be here today if it were not for the mobilization of the cultural sector. We are faced with a series of urgent measures not to leave anyone behind at this difficult time. The cultural sector has already been particularly hit in the previous economic crisis, and has in fact never recovered. That is why they are urgent measures, as I said, but not enough, and this is something we have already talked to the Minister of Culture and therefore there is a commitment to continue working, to continue putting short-term measures on the table. In the long term, we must work on cultural policies that allow us to leave behind an endemic precariousness of the sector and advance equality in a sector that still has many differences in terms of social class or gender, for example. And I say that we have to move forward on the rights of women cultural workers on a social justice issue, as is obvious, but also because this is the only way to guarantee access to a diverse culture, and I ream this word a lot: diverse. Culture is a right - it has already been said here in this rostrum - as is education or health - it is reflected in the Universal Declaration of Human Rights - but precisely because it is a right it must be ensured that this culture is as diverse as our society is. Beyond shortsighted views of culture that have been shown to have some right-wing groups, I think I am not mistaken if I say that we generally share socially that culture is an essential good; these days we have been able to see more if it can be done. Culture has accompanied us in very difficult times. Marguerite Duras said: Loneliness also means either death or the book. Well, many of us have been saved by culture these days, but culture is also an instrument that allows us to articulate critical thinking. It is a tool of individual and collective transformation and investigation; something vital always, but much more in these times of crisis in which we find ourselves. As I said, this is only the case if culture is diverse, and for this it is essential to have a healthy and non-precarious cultural ecosystem, and that is also what this royal decree is about, because these measures are aimed particularly at the most vulnerable sectors of culture. One example of this I am saying is, for example, the case of the book sector. The Royal Decree enables €4 million in aid to independent bookstores, which are obviously the ones that will be most affected by this stoppage due to the pandemic. Writer John Updike said bookstores are lonely fortresses that illuminate our streets. This Government is showing a firm commitment to the rich map of our country's bookstores. Here we have a first step and we have to continue working in the same direction. I would also like to take this opportunity to make an appeal to the citizens: let us buy in the neighbourhood bookstores, in the small bookstores of our peoples; their survival depends on all of us. I'm done, President, just like I started. Those who crave hatred, the enemies of love, are also the enemies of culture, because they precisely know that culture is a powerful instrument of reflection to build a more supportive, cohesive and just world. The feminist Audrey Lorde said: Poetry is not just a dream and a vision, it is the architecture of our lives, it lays the foundations for a future of change, a bridge that crosses our fears of what we had never been before. Let us take care of our culture, protect its diversity, think of it as a fundamental right and commend our poets, give them a dignified life. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]
